studio, drafting proposals, emailing people, and fussing with supplies because sitting quietly felt almost impossible.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem had been missing for decades, and I felt oddly certain I was the one person who could pull it off.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep finish a plate for a contract and then a bird at the window, a hardware catalog, or a half-formed idea for a workshop would pull me completely off course.\n- key: S040\n  name: stealing my work only showed up during this keyed-up stretch with the studio; before this, I might worry about contracts, but I didn’t believe people were plotting against me. I overcommitted, and I spent money I really needed to keep the studio secure, so now the mortgage feels like it’s at risk.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I worry about the deadlines and money, but I haven’t noticed myself doing any specific behavior over and over or having some thought I can’t stop repeating like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations because of your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m avoiding things exactly. I’ve been putting some tasks off because I feel overwhelmed, but it’s more that I’m behind and unsure where to start, not that I’m deliberately staying away from certain situations.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or habits?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t felt an urge like that. I don’t have rituals I feel I have to do or habits I’m driven to repeat. It’s more just general stress, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a specific stressful event or change in your life that you think might have triggered your current feelings of anxiety and overwhelm?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think it started about three months ago, when I took on the mortgage for the cottage-studio. That’s been the big change, and since then I’ve felt more anxious and overwhelmed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It seems to come up more when I’m thinking about what I owe or what I haven’t finished. When I’m not focused on that, I can feel a bit calmer.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mainly about not meeting expectations or is it more about a general feeling of worry that's not focused on anything specific?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly tied to expectations, I think. I’ve taken on too many projects—workshops, studio improvements, client calls—and I can’t realistically keep up with all of it. So the worry is mostly about the deadlines and the mortgage starting to close in on me.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety triggered by thinking about the mortgage or deadlines, or does it occur even when you're not directly thinking about them?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to say exactly. I notice it most when I’m thinking about the mortgage or deadlines, but I haven’t really tracked whether it happens completely on its own. I just know those are the things that seem to set it off the most.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if your mortgage and deadline issues were resolved?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would probably ease up some. A lot of why I came in is that I’ve taken on way more than I can realistically handle—projects, workshops, fixing up the studio, client calls—and now all those deadlines, plus the mortgage, feel like they’re closing in. If those were settled, I think I’d feel less anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you weren't facing these deadline and mortgage issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d be nearly as anxious if those things weren’t hanging over me. The main problem is that I’ve taken on too much—too many projects, workshops, studio fixes, client calls—and I can’t keep up with all of it. So if the deadlines and mortgage weren’t pressing on me, I think a lot of the anxiety would settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety started right after you took on the mortgage or was there a delay between taking on the mortgage and when you started feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: It started pretty soon after I took on the mortgage for the cottage-studio. It’s been going on about three months now, so I don’t think there was much of a delay.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’ve been putting some tasks off because I feel overwhelmed, but it’s more that I’m behind and unsure where to start, not that I’m deliberately staying away from certain situations.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or habits?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t felt an urge like that. It seems to come up more when I’m thinking about what I owe or what I haven’t finished. When I’m not focused on that, I can feel a bit calmer.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mainly about not meeting expectations or is it more about a general feeling of worry that's not focused on anything specific?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly tied to expectations, I think. When I’m not focused on that, I can feel a bit calmer.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mainly about not meeting expectations or is it more about a general feeling of worry that's not focused on anything specific?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly tied to expectations, I think. I’ve taken on too many projects—workshops, studio improvements, client calls—and I can’t realistically keep up with all of it. I just know those are the things that seem to set it off the most.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if your mortgage and deadline issues were resolved?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would probably ease up some. A lot of why I came in is that I’ve taken on way more than I can realistically handle—projects, workshops, fixing up the studio, client calls—and now all those deadlines, plus the mortgage, feel like they’re closing in. If those were settled, I think I’d feel less anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you weren't facing these deadline and mortgage issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d be nearly as anxious if those things weren’t hanging over me. The main problem is that I’ve taken on too much—too many projects, workshops, studio fixes, client calls—and I can’t keep up with all of it. So if the deadlines and mortgage weren’t pressing on me, I think a lot of the anxiety would settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety started right after you took on the mortgage or was there a delay between taking on the mortgage and when you started feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: It started pretty soon after I took on the mortgage for the cottage-studio. It’s been going on about three months now, so I don’t think there was much of a delay.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be gone completely if the mortgage and deadline issues were resolved today?\n\nPatient: Maybe not completely. Even if the mortgage and deadlines were fixed today, I think I’d still have this sort of humming current in me. From the morning on, I keep wanting to rearrange the studio, draft proposals, email people, fuss with supplies—just keep moving. Sitting quietly feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this feeling of needing to keep moving and being unable to sit quietly is related to your worries about the mortgage and deadlines?\n\nPatient: Some of it might be, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that. It’s like there’s this current running in me from the morning on. I’ll keep rearranging the studio, drafting things, emailing people, fussing with supplies—almost anything—because just sitting still feels really hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt the need to keep moving and be active, or is this something that has developed recently?\n\nPatient: It’s more recent. I mean, I’ve always been active with my work, but this feels different. Lately I’ve had this humming current in me from the morning on, and I keep doing things in the studio—rearranging, drafting proposals, emailing people, fussing with supplies—because sitting quietly feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt the need to keep moving and be active, or is this something that has developed over the past few months?\n\nPatient: It’s developed more recently, over the past few months. I’ve had this humming current in me from the morning on, and I end up rearranging the studio, drafting proposals, emailing people, fussing with supplies—just anything to keep moving, because sitting quietly feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to keep moving and be active if your anxiety about the mortgage and deadlines were completely gone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I probably would. The mortgage and deadlines add to it, but this feels more like a humming current in me from the morning on. I keep finding things to do in the studio—rearranging, drafting proposals, emailing, fussing with supplies—because sitting quietly feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this feeling of needing to keep moving would go away if you weren't anxious about anything?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it would completely go away. The anxiety makes it worse, but even without it, there’s this humming feeling in me from the morning on. I keep rearranging the studio or emailing people or fussing with supplies because sitting still feels almost impossible.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
